		<title>Build a no-code ML workflow with Snowflake, Amazon SageMaker Canvas and Amazon Quick – Part 2: Data preparation and model building with Amazon SageMaker Canvas</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-no-code-ml-workflow-with-snowflake-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-and-amazon-quick-part-2-data-preparation-and-model-building-with-amazon-sagemaker-canvas/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Anu Kaggadasapura Nagaraja]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 20 Aug 2026 21:23:29 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker Canvas]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">b4b1b2f1e88dd9dc5407ae1305eefc9e70d6e130</guid>

					<description>In Part 2 of this no-code ML series, you connect Amazon SageMaker Canvas to Snowflake, prepare and join transaction data with Data Wrangler visual transformations, and train an XGBoost fraud detection model. All without writing machine learning code, laying the groundwork for interactive dashboards in Part 3.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-no-code-ml-workflow-with-snowflake-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-and-amazon-quick-part-1-setting-up-your-snowflake-environment/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; covered the Snowflake database setup and established the foundational infrastructure for this no-code machine learning (ML) workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Part 2 of this blog series covers complete data preparation and model building workflow using Amazon SageMaker Canvas, demonstrating how to connect directly to Snowflake data sources, transform and prepare data using Data Wrangler’s visual transformations, and build a fraud detection model using the XGBoost algorithm.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/canvas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Canvas&lt;/a&gt; is a visual, no-code machine learning service that enables business analysts and domain experts to build accurate ML models and generate predictions. Amazon SageMaker Canvas provides an intuitive interface for data preparation, model training, and prediction generation democratizing access to machine learning across organizations while maintaining enterprise security and governance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This solution guides you through the complete workflow of preparing data and building a machine learning model using Amazon SageMaker Canvas, with direct integration to your Snowflake data warehouse.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Complete &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-no-code-ml-workflow-with-snowflake-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-and-amazon-quick-part-1-setting-up-your-snowflake-environment/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; setting up your Snowflake environment before starting this post. You need the Snowflake account credentials and connection details from Part 1 to complete the steps in this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-sagemaker-canvas-setup"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Canvas setup&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Open the AWS Management Console and search for Amazon SageMaker Canvas. Select it from the list, or press Enter.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="create-your-amazon-sagemaker-domain"&gt;Create your Amazon SageMaker domain&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A domain provides the foundational organizational unit for your Amazon SageMaker environment. It serves as a dedicated workspace that houses user profiles, storage configurations, and security settings. Each domain delivers isolated resources and access controls for managing team collaboration and data governance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate to environment configurations in the left pane and choose &lt;strong&gt;Domains&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create domain&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/onboard-quick-start.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Set up for single user (Quick setup&lt;/a&gt;) to automatically create both your domain and user profile.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="launch-amazon-sagemaker-canvas"&gt;Launch Amazon SageMaker Canvas&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Canvas&lt;/strong&gt; from the left-hand pane.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose the domain and user profile you created.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Open Canvas&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Wait 3–5 minutes while Canvas prepares your workspace.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-1.jpg" alt="Amazon SageMaker Canvas workspace loading after opening it from the domain" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Amazon SageMaker Canvas workspace loading after opening it from the domain&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-wrangler-ml-data-preparation"&gt;Data Wrangler: ML data preparation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/data-wrangler/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler&lt;/a&gt; simplifies data preparation for machine learning workflows. With built-in transformations and an intuitive visual interface, Data Wrangler reduces the time traditionally spent on data preparation and analysis. For industries ranging from financial services to healthcare, this capability unlocks significant value so subject matter experts can directly prepare their data for analysis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The integration with Snowflake reduces data movement challenges, so users can connect directly to their Snowflake data warehouses, transform the data within Canvas, and proceed straight to model building. This unified, no-code environment accelerates time-to-insight while maintaining data governance and security.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a.-data-connection-and-initial-setup"&gt;A. Data connection and initial setup&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this section, you connect Amazon SageMaker Canvas to a Snowflake data source.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate to Amazon SageMaker Canvas and choose &lt;strong&gt;Data Wrangler&lt;/strong&gt; from the left navigation pane. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Import and prepare&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Tabular&lt;/strong&gt; to work with structured datasets.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-2.jpg" alt="Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler Import and prepare screen with Tabular selected" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler Import and prepare screen with Tabular selected&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="2" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Specify the source of your tabular dataset. From the &lt;strong&gt;data source menu&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Snowflake&lt;/strong&gt; as your connection type. Then choose &lt;strong&gt;Add Connection&lt;/strong&gt; to establish the link between Amazon SageMaker Canvas and your Snowflake environment. With this integration, you can access your cloud data warehouse directly within Canvas. It avoids manual data exports and makes sure you are always working with the most current data in your Snowflake instance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-3.jpg" alt="Selecting Snowflake as the data source and adding a connection in Data Wrangler" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Selecting Snowflake as the data source and adding a connection in Data Wrangler&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Snowflake connection pop-up menu, provide the following: 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;A connection name.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Account ID&lt;/strong&gt;, set to your Snowflake organization value, plus a hyphen, plus the Snowflake account ID.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The username for the Snowflake account you set up earlier.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The password that you set previously.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-4.png" alt="Snowflake connection dialog with connection name, account ID, username, and password fields" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: Snowflake connection dialog with connection name, account ID, username, and password fields&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After the connection is established, you will create card-level outlier thresholds to identify unusual spending patterns for each credit card and category combination. This helps the model detect when a transaction amount significantly deviates from a cardholder’s typical behavior. Copy the SQL query to prepare the fraud detection dataset.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;select
CC_NUM,
CATEGORY,
avg(AMT) + (3* stddev_pop(AMT)) as amt_outlier,
case when sum(is_fraud)&amp;gt;1 then 1 else 0 end as fraud_history
from
FRAUD.PUBLIC.FRAUD_TABLE
where trans_date_trans_time&amp;lt;'2020-12-01'
group by
CC_NUM,
CATEGORY&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="5" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run the query: 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Edit in SQL&lt;/strong&gt; in the top right corner to open the SQL editor.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Paste the SQL query into the editor.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Run SQL&lt;/strong&gt; to run and preview results.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Import&lt;/strong&gt; to bring the dataset into your Canvas workspace.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-5.jpg" alt="SQL editor in Data Wrangler showing the outlier-threshold query results ready to import" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: SQL editor in Data Wrangler showing the outlier-threshold query results ready to import&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="b.-data-enrichment"&gt;B. Data enrichment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the following steps, you will enrich the dataset with transaction-level features and merchant-specific fraud indicators.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Data flow&lt;/strong&gt; to add more data. In the top right corner, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add Data&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Tabular&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-6.jpg" alt="Data flow canvas with the Add Data and Tabular options for adding a second source" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: Data flow canvas with the Add Data and Tabular options for adding a second source&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="2" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose the &lt;strong&gt;data source&lt;/strong&gt; as Snowflake and the existing connection you created before.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-7.jpg" alt="Selecting the existing Snowflake connection for the second data source" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 7: Selecting the existing Snowflake connection for the second data source&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the &lt;strong&gt;Edit SQL&lt;/strong&gt; window, paste the SQL query. This query provides temporal patterns (hour of day, day of week), demographic information (age categories), and merchant-level outlier thresholds, all critical signals for fraud detection. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Run SQL&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Import&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;select
    t.MERCHANT,
    t.CC_NUM,
    'H'||extract(hour from t.TRANS_DATE_TRANS_TIME) as TRANS_HOUR,
    'D'||extract(dow from t.TRANS_DATE_TRANS_TIME) as TRANS_DOW,
    t.state,
    t.gender,
    'A'||FLOOR(DATEDIFF(YEAR, DATEADD(DAY, 1, CAST(t.dob AS DATE)), CAST(t.TRANS_DATE_TRANS_TIME AS DATE)) / 5) * 5 AS age_category,
    t.CATEGORY,
    t.IS_FRAUD,
    t.AMT,
    m.merchant_amt_outlier,
    m.merchant_fraud_history
from
    FRAUD.PUBLIC.FRAUD_TABLE t
left join (
    select
        MERCHANT,
        avg(AMT) + (3* stddev_pop(AMT)) as merchant_amt_outlier,
        case when sum(is_fraud)&amp;gt;1 then 1 else 0 end as merchant_fraud_history
    from
        FRAUD.PUBLIC.FRAUD_TABLE
    where trans_date_trans_time&amp;lt;'2020-12-01'
    group by MERCHANT
) m on t.MERCHANT = m.MERCHANT
where t.trans_date_trans_time&amp;lt;'2020-12-01'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-8.png" alt="Data Wrangler preview of the enrichment query with temporal, demographic, and merchant features" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 8: Data Wrangler preview of the enrichment query with temporal, demographic, and merchant features&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="c.-data-transformation"&gt;C. Data transformation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this section, you will join the data together.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the &lt;strong&gt;Data flow&lt;/strong&gt; screen, you see the two data sources imported from Snowflake. Choose the first &lt;strong&gt;data type&lt;/strong&gt; and choose the plus icon next to it. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Combine data&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Join&lt;/strong&gt;. When the right panel opens, choose the second &lt;strong&gt;data type&lt;/strong&gt; from the data flow to join.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-9.jpg" alt="Data flow showing the Combine data and Join options between the two Snowflake sources" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 9: Data flow showing the Combine data and Join options between the two Snowflake sources&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="2" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Left outer join&lt;/strong&gt; for the &lt;strong&gt;Join type&lt;/strong&gt;. Add a &lt;strong&gt;Join key&lt;/strong&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Category&lt;/strong&gt; as the left and right join key.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-10.jpg" alt="Join configuration with Left outer join type and Category as the join key" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 10: Join configuration with Left outer join type and Category as the join key&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Preview&lt;/strong&gt; to view the joined data. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Add&lt;/strong&gt; to proceed.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-11.jpg" alt="Preview of the joined dataset before adding it to the data flow" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 11: Preview of the joined dataset before adding it to the data flow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next, you will create outlier features and remove sensitive columns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Return to the &lt;strong&gt;Data flow&lt;/strong&gt; screen, choose the join, choose the plus sign (&lt;strong&gt;+&lt;/strong&gt;) next to it, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Add transform&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-12.jpg" alt="Adding a transform to the join node in the data flow" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 12: Adding a transform to the join node in the data flow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="5" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After the interface opens, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add transform&lt;/strong&gt; in the right-hand panel, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Custom Formula&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-13.jpg" alt="Choosing Custom Formula in the Add transform panel" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 13: Choosing Custom Formula in the Add transform panel&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="6" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;This option gives you the flexibility to add columns using a Spark SQL expression. Copy and paste the following &lt;strong&gt;Formula&lt;/strong&gt; on the Home tab. Enter the output column as &lt;code&gt;CC_FLAG&lt;/code&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Add&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CASE WHEN AMT &amp;gt; AMT_OUTLIER THEN 1 ELSE 0 END&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-14.jpg" alt="Custom formula creating the CC_FLAG column from the card outlier threshold" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 14: Custom formula creating the CC_FLAG column from the card outlier threshold&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="7" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To add another column, repeat the same steps to enter a custom formula, then copy and paste this query and name the output column as &lt;code&gt;MERCHANT_AMT_FLAG&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CASE WHEN AMT &amp;gt; MERCHANT_AMT_OUTLIER THEN 1 ELSE 0 END&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-15.jpg" alt="Custom formula creating the MERCHANT_AMT_FLAG column from the merchant outlier threshold" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 15: Custom formula creating the MERCHANT_AMT_FLAG column from the merchant outlier threshold&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="8" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To make sure the model remains free of sensitive information such as card numbers, merchant names, and outlier amounts tied to cards or merchants, remove these columns using built-in transformation. 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Add transform&lt;/strong&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Manage Columns&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Select the &lt;strong&gt;Transform&lt;/strong&gt; type to &lt;strong&gt;Drop column&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Select the columns you want to remove from your dataset&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Add&lt;/strong&gt; to apply the transformation. 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;CC_NUM&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;AMT_OUTLIER&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;MERCHANT&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;MERCHANT_AMT_OUTLIER&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-16.jpg" alt="Manage Columns transform dropping sensitive columns from the dataset" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 16: Manage Columns transform dropping sensitive columns from the dataset&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="d.-quality-analysis-and-model-export"&gt;D. Quality analysis and model export&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With data preparation complete, you will run a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/data-wrangler-data-insights.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;quality analysis report&lt;/a&gt; to get insights into the data. The insights report identifies common data issues, such as target leakage or class imbalance, helping users address them early in the workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To initiate the analysis, choose the &lt;strong&gt;Analyses&lt;/strong&gt; tab. In the right-hand panel, choose &lt;strong&gt;Data Quality and Insights Report&lt;/strong&gt; from the &lt;strong&gt;Analysis type&lt;/strong&gt; list.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;code&gt;IS_FRAUD&lt;/code&gt; as the &lt;strong&gt;target column&lt;/strong&gt;. This tells Canvas which variable you want to predict. Then choose the &lt;strong&gt;Classification&lt;/strong&gt; option under &lt;strong&gt;Problem type&lt;/strong&gt;. The &lt;strong&gt;Data size&lt;/strong&gt; should remain as &lt;strong&gt;Sampled Dataset&lt;/strong&gt;. Finally, choose &lt;strong&gt;Create&lt;/strong&gt; to launch the analysis.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-17.jpg" alt="Data Quality and Insights Report configuration with IS_FRAUD target and Classification problem type" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 17: Data Quality and Insights Report configuration with IS_FRAUD target and Classification problem type&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Within a few minutes, a detailed analysis report will be created which includes a quick summary of the data, feature summary, duplicate rows, anomalous samples and much more. In the Quick model section, review the accuracy metrics in the training and validation datasets. A confusion matrix follows the accuracy statistics. The idea is to use this report after any data engineering to observe how it impacts model quality.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-18.jpg" alt="Data quality and insights report with Quick model accuracy metrics and confusion matrix" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 18: Data quality and insights report with Quick model accuracy metrics and confusion matrix&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The feature summary section shows feature importance. In practice, if there are features with low prediction power, you might choose to drop those features.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-19.png" alt="Feature summary section of the insights report showing feature importance" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 19: Feature summary section of the insights report showing feature importance&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="export-to-model-building"&gt;Export to model building&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You’ve now combined two data sources, engineered new features, removed unnecessary ones, and previewed your model’s potential accuracy by running the analysis. With data preparation complete, it’s time to &lt;strong&gt;build your predictive model.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To begin, return to the &lt;strong&gt;Data flow&lt;/strong&gt; tab, choose the plus sign (&lt;strong&gt;+&lt;/strong&gt;) next to your final transform, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Create model&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-20.jpg" alt="Creating a model from the final transform node in the data flow" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 20: Creating a model from the final transform node in the data flow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="2" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose a descriptive name under &lt;strong&gt;Model name&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Export and create model&lt;/strong&gt;. The export process may take a few minutes as Canvas processes your entire dataset in real time.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-21.jpg" alt="Model name entry and the Export and create model action in Canvas" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 21: Model name entry and the Export and create model action in Canvas&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After a few minutes, the &lt;strong&gt;Build&lt;/strong&gt; screen opens up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-22.jpg" alt="Canvas Build screen after exporting the prepared dataset" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 22: Canvas Build screen after exporting the prepared dataset&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;code&gt;IS_FRAUD&lt;/code&gt; as the &lt;strong&gt;Target Column&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Configure model&lt;/strong&gt; under &lt;strong&gt;Model type.&lt;/strong&gt; Select &lt;strong&gt;2-category model&lt;/strong&gt; as &lt;strong&gt;model type&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-23.jpg" alt="Configuring a 2-category model type in Canvas" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 23: Configuring a 2-category model type in Canvas&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="5" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Next, select &lt;strong&gt;Ensemble&lt;/strong&gt; as the training method with &lt;strong&gt;XGBoost&lt;/strong&gt; as the algorithm, a strategic choice that balances accuracy with efficiency.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-24.jpg" alt="Selecting the Ensemble training method with the XGBoost algorithm" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 24: Selecting the Ensemble training method with the XGBoost algorithm&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="6" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deselect the &lt;strong&gt;FRAUD_HISTORY&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;MERCHANT_FRAUD_HISTORY&lt;/strong&gt; columns, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Standard build&lt;/strong&gt; to start training. The model takes approximately 15–30 minutes to complete.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-25.jpg" alt="Deselecting history columns and starting a Standard build" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 25: Deselecting history columns and starting a Standard build&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="7" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;With the model training complete, navigate to the &lt;strong&gt;Analyze&lt;/strong&gt; tab to review the results. Here, you can examine which features had the most impact on predictions and explore the scatterplot and charts to understand relationships between data values and fraud classification.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-26.jpg" alt="Canvas Analyze tab showing feature impact and fraud classification charts" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 26: Canvas Analyze tab showing feature impact and fraud classification charts&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="8" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Advanced Metrics&lt;/strong&gt; to further understand model performance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-27.jpg" alt="Advanced Metrics view of the trained fraud detection model" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 27: Advanced Metrics view of the trained fraud detection model&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="9" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Next, use the trained model to make predictions on an unseen dataset. Download the &lt;a href="https://aws-blogs-artifacts-public.s3.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/artifacts/ML-20655/prediction_dataset.csv" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample prediction CSV file.&lt;/a&gt; Navigate to the &lt;strong&gt;Predict&lt;/strong&gt; tab. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Manual&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Create Dataset&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-28.jpg" alt="Predict tab with Manual dataset creation for generating predictions" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 28: Predict tab with Manual dataset creation for generating predictions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="10" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Upload the dataset, choose &lt;strong&gt;Preview dataset&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Create dataset&lt;/strong&gt;. After the dataset loads, choose the dataset and choose &lt;strong&gt;Generate Predictions&lt;/strong&gt;. The model takes a few minutes to make the predictions. Wait until the status of the job changes to &lt;strong&gt;Ready&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To analyze the results visually using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/quicksight/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/a&gt;, you must first verify the following prerequisites (detailed &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/canvas-send-predictions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;here&lt;/a&gt;): 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Verify AWS Region alignment: Your Quick Sight account must be set up in the same AWS Region as your Amazon SageMaker Canvas domain.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Add Amazon Quick Sight permissions to your Amazon SageMaker execution role: The AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) execution role attached to your Amazon SageMaker domain needs additional permissions to send predictions to Amazon Quick Sight. Navigate to the IAM console, find the execution role associated with your Amazon SageMaker domain (created during &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/onboard-quick-start.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;setting up Amazon SageMaker domain&lt;/a&gt; at the very beginning), and add the required inline policy as described &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/canvas-quicksight-permissions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;here&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Grant Quick Sight access to the Amazon SageMaker S3 bucket: Navigate to Quick Sight, go to &lt;strong&gt;Manage Accounts&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;AWS Resources&lt;/strong&gt; from the left-hand navigation pane. Make sure Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) is selected and choose the appropriate S3 bucket that has the predictions generated by Amazon SageMaker Canvas, named sagemaker-{region}-{account_id}.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Add Quick Sight users: Make sure the users you want to share predictions with have been added to your Quick Sight account with an &lt;strong&gt;Author&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Admin&lt;/strong&gt; role. Go to &lt;strong&gt;Manage Quick Sight&lt;/strong&gt; and navigate to &lt;strong&gt;Manage users&lt;/strong&gt; to invite new users or verify existing ones. For details, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/managing-user-access-qs-iam.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Managing user access&lt;/a&gt;. You will enter their usernames when sending predictions.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Next, select the &lt;strong&gt;Job name&lt;/strong&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Send to Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-29.jpg" alt="Selecting the prediction job and sending results to Amazon Quick Sight" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 29: Selecting the prediction job and sending results to Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="13" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the new window, add the users who were previously granted Amazon Quick Sight permissions as viewers of the dashboard, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Send&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P2-30.jpg" alt="Adding dashboard viewers before sending predictions to Amazon Quick Sight" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 30: Adding dashboard viewers before sending predictions to Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Part 2 covered the complete data preparation and model building workflow in Amazon SageMaker Canvas from connecting to Snowflake data sources and engineering features using Data Wrangler’s visual transformations, to joining multiple data sources, analyzing data quality, and training a fraud detection model all without requiring machine learning programming expertise. With the trained model now generating predictions on unseen data, the foundation is set for &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-no-code-ml-workflow-with-snowflake-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-and-amazon-quick-part-3-visualizing-insights-with-amazon-quick-sight/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 3&lt;/a&gt;, where those ML-driven insights are brought to life through interactive dashboards in Amazon Quick Sight.&lt;/p&gt;

				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>

		<guid isPermaLink="false">bb73a3f10f6755b65e14b7bb5975900fe1671817</guid>

					<description>In Part 3 of this no-code ML series, you bring fraud detection predictions to life. Import your Amazon SageMaker Canvas predictions into Amazon Quick Sight, build interactive dashboards, use generative BI to answer questions in natural language, and publish AI-generated executive summaries for stakeholders.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-no-code-ml-workflow-with-snowflake-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-and-amazon-quick-part-1-setting-up-your-snowflake-environment/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; covered the Snowflake database implementation setup and established the foundational infrastructure for our no-code machine learning (ML) workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-no-code-ml-workflow-with-snowflake-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-and-amazon-quick-part-2-data-preparation-and-model-building-with-amazon-sagemaker-canvas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 2&lt;/a&gt; walked through the complete data preparation and model building workflow using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/canvas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Canvas&lt;/a&gt;, demonstrating how to connect directly to Snowflake data sources, transform and prepare data using Data Wrangler visual transformations, and build a fraud detection model using the XGBoost algorithm.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In Part 3, the workflow comes full circle by integrating &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/canvas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Canvas&lt;/a&gt; predictions with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksight/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Sight,&lt;/a&gt; now part of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick,&lt;/a&gt; to create interactive dashboards that combine operational data with ML predictions for fraud detection business intelligence (BI). This post covers how to import Canvas predictions into Amazon Quick Sight as a dataset, build an analysis dashboard, use generative BI capabilities to surface insights through natural language, and publish those insights to stakeholders.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="building-dashboards-with-amazon-quick-sight"&gt;Building dashboards with Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/strong&gt; is a powerful business intelligence service within &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/strong&gt; that teams can use to build interactive dashboards, perform deep data analysis, and share insights across their organization. Amazon Quick extends these capabilities with generative AI that supports natural language queries over your data, custom AI agents for workflow automation, and collaborative Spaces for organizing files, dashboards, and knowledge bases in one place.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For teams already using Amazon SageMaker Canvas, Amazon Quick provides a direct path from ML predictions to business-ready dashboards without requiring additional infrastructure or custom integrations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section walks through the complete workflow for visualizing fraud detection predictions in Amazon Quick Sight, from importing your Amazon SageMaker Canvas predictions as a dataset to building interactive dashboards and leveraging generative BI capabilities for natural language insights.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before starting Part 3, complete the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-no-code-ml-workflow-with-snowflake-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-and-amazon-quick-part-1-setting-up-your-snowflake-environment/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Part 1&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/a&gt; – Set up a Snowflake account, create the fraud detection database and table, and retrieve the Snowflake organization account name needed for &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/canvas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Canvas&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-no-code-ml-workflow-with-snowflake-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-and-amazon-quick-part-2-data-preparation-and-model-building-with-amazon-sagemaker-canvas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Part 2&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/a&gt; – Connect Amazon SageMaker Canvas to Snowflake, prepare the dataset using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/data-wrangler/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Data Wrangler&lt;/a&gt;, build and train the XGBoost fraud detection model, and send predictions to Amazon Quick Sight.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Sign up for Amazon Quick to facilitate access to Amazon Quick Sight, and upgrade users to Admin Pro, Author Pro, or Reader Pro for generative BI capabilities.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="import-canvas-predictions-as-a-dataset"&gt;Import Canvas predictions as a dataset&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before building visualizations, import the Canvas predictions into Amazon Quick Sight as a dataset:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the previous part of this series, Amazon SageMaker Canvas predictions were sent to Amazon Quick Sight. This dataset will be available on Amazon Quick Sight’s console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;From the left navigation pane, choose &lt;strong&gt;Datasets&lt;/strong&gt; to locate your Canvas predictions dataset.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create analysis&lt;/strong&gt;. This opens a blank canvas where you can begin building visualizations that transform your fraud detection predictions into actionable insights.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="build-visualizations"&gt;Build visualizations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the dataset imported, the next step is creating visuals to analyze the predictions:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the analysis interface, start by selecting fields from your dataset to create your first visual. For detailed guidance on creating dashboards, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/latest/userguide/working-with-an-analysis.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Sight analysis creation.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The following is the analysis dashboard created showcasing fraud patterns across multiple dimensions including transaction categories, merchant behavior, and temporal patterns.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P3-1.png" alt="Fraud detection analysis dashboard showing fraud patterns across transaction categories, merchant behavior, and temporal trends" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Fraud detection analysis dashboard showing fraud patterns across transaction categories, merchant behavior, and temporal trends&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The generative BI capabilities of Amazon Quick Sight accelerate dashboard creation through natural language interactions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To access Generative BI capabilities in Amazon Quick Sight, upgrade your account’s users to &lt;strong&gt;Admin Pro&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Author Pro&lt;/strong&gt;, or &lt;strong&gt;Reader Pro&lt;/strong&gt; roles within your Amazon Quick subscription. Pro roles grant access to the generative BI capabilities relevant to each assigned role, including the ability to share generative Q&amp;amp;A topics with other users. To understand how subscription names map to user roles, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/user-types.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Understanding Amazon Quick subscriptions and roles&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose the sparkle icon. In the pane that opens, the chat displays context-aware options based on your current task. When working in an analysis, you can build calculations, create and edit visuals, set up Q&amp;amp;A topics, or ask questions about your data directly.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P3-2.png" alt="Amazon Quick Sight generative BI pane with context-aware options for building calculations, visuals, and Q&amp;amp;A topics" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Amazon Quick Sight generative BI pane with context-aware options for building calculations, visuals, and Q&amp;amp;A topics&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="5" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/generative-bi-build-visuals.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;generate a custom visual&lt;/a&gt;, enter a natural language description of the that you want outcome in the &lt;strong&gt;Build a visual&lt;/strong&gt; menu. Review the generated visual and choose &lt;strong&gt;Add to analysis&lt;/strong&gt; to include it in your dashboard.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the following example, a visual of fraud rate by transaction hour is built using a simple natural language query which reveals how fraudulent activity fluctuates across different times of day, surfacing temporal patterns that would be difficult to detect through manual analysis alone.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;

 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Generated visual of fraud rate by transaction hour created from a natural language query&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="7" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To query the dashboard directly without building a new visual, enter your question in the generative BI pane to get an instant, AI-powered answer. In the following example, the generative BI capabilities responds to a simple query of total fraud cases in the state of Washington and instantly returns the result, demonstrating how natural language questions can surface data-driven answers for business users.&lt;/li&gt;

 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: Generative BI answer returning total fraud cases in Washington state from a natural language question&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;h3 id="publish-the-dashboard"&gt;Publish the dashboard&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To publish the dashboard, complete the following steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After all modifications to the analysis are complete, choose &lt;strong&gt;Publish&lt;/strong&gt; in the top corner of the analysis.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P3-5.png" alt="Publishing an analysis in Amazon Quick Sight using the Publish option" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: Publishing an analysis in Amazon Quick Sight using the Publish option&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="2" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the dialog box that appears, enter a descriptive name for the dashboard in the &lt;strong&gt;Dashboard name&lt;/strong&gt; field. Verify that the &lt;strong&gt;Allow executive summary&lt;/strong&gt; option is selected. This enables AI-powered executive summaries for dashboard viewers. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Publish dashboard&lt;/strong&gt; to complete the publishing process.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P3-6.png" alt="Publish dashboard dialog with a dashboard name and the Allow executive summary option selected" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: Publish dashboard dialog with a dashboard name and the Allow executive summary option selected&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_137641" style="width: 2890px" class="wp-caption alignleft"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-137641" loading="lazy" class="wp-image-137641 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/20/Quick-image.png" alt="" width="2880" height="1496"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-137641" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: From the published dashboard, users can generate an executive summary using the Create option&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After the dashboard is published, users can access additional capabilities from the top menu bar: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Share&lt;/strong&gt; – Control dashboard access and permissions for users and groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Send reports&lt;/strong&gt; – Schedule automated report delivery via email.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Threshold alerts&lt;/strong&gt; – Configure notifications when metrics exceed defined thresholds.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Export&lt;/strong&gt; – Download dashboard content as PDF files for offline distribution.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create executive summary&lt;/strong&gt; – Generate AI-powered insights from dashboard data.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In addition to these, there are other capabilities available to help users interact with, customize, and share their dashboards, all of which are covered in detail in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/using-dashboards.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick dashboard documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To generate an executive summary, choose &lt;strong&gt;Create&lt;/strong&gt; in the top menu bar and choose &lt;strong&gt;Executive summary&lt;/strong&gt; from the menu.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The executive summary uses generative AI to automatically analyze your visualizations and provide natural language insights, making it more straightforward for stakeholders to quickly understand the most important findings.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-20655P3-8.png" alt="AI-generated executive summary providing natural language insights from the dashboard" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 8: AI-generated executive summary providing natural language insights from the dashboard&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The fraud detection dashboard brings together Amazon SageMaker Canvas predictions and Amazon Quick Sight to surface fraud patterns across transaction categories, merchant behavior, and temporal trends all in one interactive view. The published dashboard further enables automated report scheduling, threshold-based alerts, and AI-generated executive summaries, empowering both technical and non-technical audiences to make faster, data-driven decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With generative BI, stakeholders can query insights in natural language and access AI-generated executive summaries, closing the loop on the end-to-end no-code ML workflow built across this series.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This three-part series demonstrates how AWS services can work together to take organizations from raw data to actionable business intelligence entirely through visual, no-code tooling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For organizations managing large volumes of operational data, this workflow removes the traditional barriers between data science and business decision-making. Insights that once required specialized engineering resources are now accessible to analysts, operations teams, and executives through a governed, scalable, and fully managed AWS architecture. Whether the goal is detecting anomalies, forecasting demand, classifying risk, or understanding customer behavior, this approach scales across use cases and industries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To start transforming your Snowflake data into actionable business intelligence, explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/canvas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Canvas&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksight/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Authoring Dogwood policies from natural language in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/authoring-dogwood-policies-from-natural-language-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sandesh Swamy]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 20 Aug 2026 16:31:28 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">4f766a78e962cad52bb901c5ca408d902b8d04c5</guid>

					<description>AI agents can take actions that do not match your organization's policies. Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore lets teams enforce controls across agents, now including time-based constraints. This post shows how Policy Authoring turns natural-language policy documents into correct Dogwood policies, with worked examples and best practices.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;AI agents can automate complex workflows but might take actions that don’t align with your organization’s policies or regulatory constraints if used without proper controls. To address this, we built &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; so teams can implement controls that are applied across agents running in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;. This was recently expanded with new capabilities for enforcing restrictions that constrain agent actions &lt;em&gt;across time&lt;/em&gt;, which support policies such as rate limiting, prerequisites and sequential ordering of tool calls, and cumulative effects. These policies are expressed in &lt;a href="https://github.com/dogwood-policy/dogwood" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dogwood&lt;/a&gt;, an open source governance language, and applied to agent actions in real time by the Dogwood monitor built into the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As part of this new launch, we expand the capabilities of &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-natural-language.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy Authoring&lt;/a&gt;, an AI-driven tool to convert natural language policy specification documents into syntactically and semantically correct Dogwood formal specifications. With this new feature, you can generate policies that enforce temporal and trajectory constraints, invoke &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; services to detect inappropriate content in the semantic meaning of free-form text, as well as policies that place restrictions on the input parameters of tools which were available in the previous version of Policy in AgentCore. Whatever your technical background, you can import policy documents written in natural language directly into the policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to safeguard your deployed agentic systems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we demonstrate this new capability using examples and provide guidance on how you can use best practices when constructing natural language policies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="automated-translation-of-natural-language-policies-to-dogwood"&gt;Automated translation of natural language policies to Dogwood&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dogwood policies can be written entirely by hand, and for a small set of controls that is a perfectly reasonable place to start. Policy Authoring works best when you already have rules written in prose and the work in front of you is transcription rather than design. You can provide a document containing a clean set of rules: a list of policies, the rules section of an operating procedure, or a written paragraph of permitted or restricted actions. Authoring is a translator rather than a summarizer, so a document that interleaves its rules with rationale, background, and commentary is better pared down to the rules themselves first.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-setting"&gt;Example setting&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To keep the examples concrete, let us consider a customer-servicing agent at a retail bank. It verifies callers, files disputes, issues refunds against disputed charges, moves funds between a customer’s own accounts, and can ask a supervisor to approve a charge. Its tools are reached through the AgentCore Gateway, and each takes a small set of arguments and returns a result:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Purpose&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Input&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Output&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;verify_identity&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Step-up verification of the caller&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ account: String }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ verified: Bool }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;initiate_transfer&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Moves funds between the customer’s accounts&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ account: String, dest_account: String,&amp;nbsp; amount: Long }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ confirmation: String }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;issue_refund&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reverses a disputed charge&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ account: String, charge_id: String, amount: Long }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ refunded: Bool }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;file_dispute&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Opens a dispute case&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ account: String, description: String }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ case_id: String }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;request_approval&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Asks a supervisor to approve a charge&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ charge_id: String }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{ approved: Bool }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Alongside the policy document, authoring takes a schema carrying exactly this information: the tool names, the arguments they accept, and the values they return. That schema is generated from the agent’s Model Context Protocol (MCP) tool manifest, so the policies that come out refer to the same names the agent actually calls. For example, &lt;code&gt;context.input.amount&lt;/code&gt; in a generated policy is the &lt;code&gt;amount&lt;/code&gt; argument in the preceding table. Authoring is also given the set of available Amazon Bedrock Guardrails checks, and identity claims that a policy is allowed to reference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The bank’s compliance team maintains its controls as a written document in the form it already uses for its human staff. The rules that follow are taken from that document, each followed by the Dogwood policy that Policy Authoring produced for it. Two conventions make the output more straightforward to read. Dogwood is default-deny and a &lt;code&gt;forbid&lt;/code&gt; overrides a &lt;code&gt;permit&lt;/code&gt;, so a rule that grants a capability becomes a &lt;code&gt;permit&lt;/code&gt; carrying the conditions, while a rule that limits or caps something becomes a &lt;code&gt;forbid&lt;/code&gt;. And a condition can examine either the call being decided or what has already happened in the same session. The following examples do both.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="policy-translation-examples"&gt;Policy translation examples&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following examples show how the autoformalizer translates natural language policies into Dogwood formulas.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-constraint-on-a-tools-arguments"&gt;A constraint on a tool’s arguments&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Refunds might be issued only during business hours, defined as 9:00 AM–5:00 PM UTC, and only for amounts of $2,500 or less.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;permit ( principal, action == AgentCore::Action::"issue_refund", resource )
when { context.system.now.toTime() &amp;gt;= duration("9h")
    &amp;amp;&amp;amp; context.system.now.toTime() &amp;lt;= duration("17h") }
when { context.input.amount &amp;lt;= 2500 };&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One sentence carrying two independent requirements becomes one policy with two conditions, both of which must hold for a refund to be permitted. &lt;code&gt;context.input.amount&lt;/code&gt; is the amount argument of the &lt;code&gt;issue_refund&lt;/code&gt; call as the agent issued it, compared in whatever units the tool declares. The document’s “$2,500” and the tool’s &lt;code&gt;amount&lt;/code&gt; need to agree on that. The time comparison reads the clock at the moment the call is decided, and neither condition depends on anything the agent did earlier. For more detail on time-based functions like &lt;code&gt;duration&lt;/code&gt;, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-time-based.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Time-based policy support&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-required-prior-step"&gt;A required prior step&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Do not initiate a transfer unless the caller’s identity has been verified for that same account within the previous 15 minutes.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;permit ( principal, action == AgentCore::Action::"initiate_transfer", resource )
when temporal {
    formerly within 15m AgentCore::Action::"verify_identity"::response{
        input.account:   context.input.account,
        output.verified: true
    }
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This rule cannot be settled from the transfer request alone, so the generated condition looks at what the agent has already done. &lt;code&gt;formerly within 15m&lt;/code&gt; asks whether the event it describes occurred at any point in the past fifteen minutes. Here, the completion of a &lt;code&gt;verify_identity&lt;/code&gt; call (&lt;code&gt;::response&lt;/code&gt;, the result, rather than the call being made) that came back with &lt;code&gt;verified: true&lt;/code&gt;. Inside an event pattern, a bare &lt;code&gt;input.account&lt;/code&gt; names a field of that earlier event, while &lt;code&gt;context.input.account&lt;/code&gt; names one on the call being decided. Setting the two equal is what makes “that same account” precise. A verification of some other account, or one that was attempted and came back unverified, does not satisfy the rule. And because the history examined is the current session’s, the rule needs no separate ID for the caller, which is why &lt;code&gt;verify_identity&lt;/code&gt; takes only the account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-cumulative-cap"&gt;A cumulative cap&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Block a transfer if the total amount transferred in the past 12 hours would exceed $50,000.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;forbid ( principal, action == AgentCore::Action::"initiate_transfer", resource )
when temporal {
    exists (total: Long). (
        (sum a for (a: Long), (t: Timepoint). where (
            formerly within 12h (
                AgentCore::Action::"initiate_transfer"::request{ input.amount: a } &amp;amp;&amp;amp; tp(t)
            )
        )) == total &amp;amp;&amp;amp; total &amp;gt; 50000
    )
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here, the history isn’t searched but added up. The policy takes the amount argument of every transfer in the past 12 hours, sums them, and denies the current call if the running total passes $50,000. Each individual transfer in that window might be small and unremarkable, but the condition instead constrains their aggregate. Note also what the document leaves open: it says “transferred” without saying whether a blocked or failed attempt counts. The translation sums &lt;code&gt;::request&lt;/code&gt; events, meaning every transfer the agent attempted, which is the safer reading for a cap. However, saying so in the document removes the guess, and that is the subject of the first best practice that follows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-rate-limit"&gt;A rate limit&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;The agent might attempt no more than three refunds against the same account within one hour.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;forbid ( principal, action == AgentCore::Action::"issue_refund", resource )
when temporal {
    exists (n: Long). (
        (count for (t: Timepoint). where (
            formerly within 1h (
                AgentCore::Action::"issue_refund"::request{ input.account: context.input.account } &amp;amp;&amp;amp; tp(t)
            )
        )) == n &amp;amp;&amp;amp; n &amp;gt; 3
    )
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This has the same shape as the previous policy, counting events rather than summing a field. The count is restricted to refunds against the account named in the call under consideration, and it includes that call, so the fourth attempt within the hour is the one that is denied. This rule says “attempt” explicitly, so unlike the previous one it leaves nothing to infer: a refund that was denied or that failed still counts against the limit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-check-on-free-form-text"&gt;A check on free-form text&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Reject any dispute filing whose description contains a Social Security number.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;forbid ( principal, action == AgentCore::Action::"file_dispute", resource )
when {
    BedrockGuardrails::SensitiveInformation(["US_SOCIAL_SECURITY_NUMBER"], [context.input.description])
        .maxConfidenceScore().greaterThanOrEqual(decimal("0.2"))
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Some rules are about the meaning of free-form text rather than a structured value, and no comparison on a field will decide them. For these, the generated policy calls an Amazon Bedrock Guardrails check inline, on the field the rule names, and compares the reported confidence against a threshold. This rule states no threshold, so the translation uses the default for that check. When a document does state one (for example, “with high confidence”, or a specific number), that value is carried through instead.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-rule-that-draws-on-more-than-one-kind-of-condition"&gt;A rule that draws on more than one kind of condition&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;A refund of more than $500 requires a supervisor’s approval for that charge, recorded within the last 30 minutes.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;forbid ( principal, action == AgentCore::Action::"issue_refund", resource )
when { context.input.amount &amp;gt; 500 }
unless temporal {
    formerly within 30m AgentCore::Action::"request_approval"::response{
        input.charge_id: context.input.charge_id,
        output.approved: true
    }
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sentence has two parts that are checked in quite different ways: a threshold on an argument of the current call, and a condition on what has already happened. Both clauses live in the same policy. The rule narrows an existing permission: it denies refunds over $500, and the &lt;code&gt;unless&lt;/code&gt; clause is the exception that lifts the denial when a matching approval is on record. As in the earlier prerequisite example, the correlation on &lt;code&gt;charge_id&lt;/code&gt; is what stops an approval for one charge from authorizing a refund on another.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices"&gt;Best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Clear and unambiguous policies result in more predictable behaviors and fewer errors, whether the implementer is a human user or an autonomous agent. As well, this improves the performance of the natural language to Dogwood authoring solution introduced earlier. Next, we review a handful of tips and best practices for constructing natural language policies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Say whether you mean the attempt or the outcome&lt;/strong&gt;. “After a transfer” is ambiguous. “After a transfer succeeds” is not. An attempt is any call the agent issued, including ones that were denied or failed. Only a completed call carries the values the tool returned. Rate limits and cumulative caps are usually about attempts, prerequisites and ordering rules about outcomes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;State the window&lt;/strong&gt;. “Recently” has no translation. “Within the past 30 minutes” does. Windows look backward from the call being decided, so if a rule is meant to reset on a calendar boundary rather than slide with the clock, state that explicitly, because it requires a different control.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Name what the rule is keyed to&lt;/strong&gt;. “No more than three transfers per hour” does not say whose: three by this caller, or three against this account? Both are expressible, they are different policies, and the sentence chooses neither. Wherever a rule counts, sums, or correlates, name the field that ties the events together.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Give the threshold and its boundary&lt;/strong&gt;. “More than three” and “at least three” differ by one action, usually the one the rule exists to stop. The same applies to the confidence levels on content checks.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Review the generated Dogwood policies for correctness&lt;/strong&gt;. Each Dogwood policy is returned alongside the sentence it came from, so that you can read the two side by side. While validation can establish that a policy is well-formed and anchored in the right schema, it doesn’t confirm that the policy says what its author meant. That judgment stays with the person who owns the document.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="knowing-what-cannot-be-enforced"&gt;Knowing what can’t be enforced&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While the authoring service can filter out and highlight policies that are incompatible with enforcement by Policy in AgentCore, you should also be aware of the common issues.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It isn’t a rule about an action&lt;/strong&gt;. “Agents should always act in the customer’s best financial interest and exercise sound professional judgment.” There’s no condition here on any action, field, or principal. This is a real requirement, and it belongs in the agent’s instructions, its evaluations, and its training, rather than in an authorization engine.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It asks for an action, not a verdict&lt;/strong&gt;. “When a dispute description contains a Social Security number, redact it before the note is stored.” A policy engine permits or denies a call. It doesn’t modify one. The neighboring rule that denies a filing containing a Social Security number is expressible and appears among the preceding examples. Redaction is a different control, applied at a different point in the pipeline.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It is outside what the language expresses&lt;/strong&gt;. “Deny wire transfers on weekends and U.S. federal bank holidays.” The date and time support in Dogwood covers points in time, offsets, and differences. There is no day-of-week accessor and no holiday calendar. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/dogwood-policy/dogwood/tree/main/dogwood-docs/guide" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dogwood language&lt;/a&gt; guide sets out in detail which constructs are available, and it is worth reading through that guidance when a rule is set aside by the policy authoring service, both to confirm the gap and to see whether a nearby formulation is supported.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It’s outside the scope of enforcement&lt;/strong&gt;. “A customer might initiate at most ten transfers per day, counted across all of that customer’s concurrent sessions.” Enforcement evaluates a trajectory within a session, so a cap that pools across sessions isn’t something a different phrasing can recover.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In each case the useful output is not a policy but a label indicating that it cannot be translated into Dogwood, which tells you that you should consider alternatives: rewrite it, move it to a different control, or accept that it stays a human process.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-policy-authoring-works"&gt;How policy authoring works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The autoformalization pipeline runs in four steps. It begins by &lt;em&gt;decomposing&lt;/em&gt; the document. Rules written for a reader are often compound: a numbered clause frequently carries several independent obligations, and a single sentence sometimes carries two, as the preceding business-hours example does. Decomposition splits them into atomic rules, each one a statement about a specific tool or set of tools that can be enforced on its own. Each rule is then &lt;em&gt;routed&lt;/em&gt;. A rule is either expressible in Dogwood and its constituent monitors, or it is not, and the ones that aren’t are set aside instead of translated, generally because of the four reasons in the previous section. Filtering here before translation attempt keeps an inexpressible rule from becoming a policy that validates cleanly and enforces the wrong thing. The rules that remain are &lt;em&gt;autoformalized&lt;/em&gt; into Dogwood, anchored in the tool schema supplied alongside the document. Finally, every candidate policy is &lt;em&gt;validated&lt;/em&gt; using the same &lt;a href="https://github.com/dogwood-policy/dogwood/tree/main/dogwood-cli" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dogwood command-line tools&lt;/a&gt; that ship with the open source language. This is the part of the pipeline that isn’t a matter of judgment: the compiler is a precise and deterministic authority on whether a policy parses and whether every name in it exists in the schema. Where a candidate is rejected, its diagnostics are handed back and the rule is translated again with those errors in view, for a bounded number of rounds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What comes out is two collections: the policies that validated for syntax and compatibility with the environment schema along with the atomic natural language rules that generated them, and the atomic natural language rules that were set aside.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post demonstrated how Policy Authoring turns a written policy document into Dogwood policies. You’ve seen examples of translations covering constraints on tool arguments, prerequisites, cumulative caps, rate limits, and checks on free-form content. As well, you’ve reviewed the properties that make a natural language rule translate well: a stated window, a named subject, an explicit threshold, and a clear choice between the attempt and the outcome. Dogwood can be written directly, and teams who prefer to work in the language are welcome to keep doing so. Authoring is there for the common case where the rules already exist in prose, to shorten the path from a document you already maintain to a set of policies you can review and deploy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt; for creating a policy engine and authoring policies from a document, and the &lt;a href="https://github.com/dogwood-policy/dogwood/tree/main/dogwood-docs/guide" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dogwood language guide&lt;/a&gt; if you would like to read or extend the generated policies yourself. For more on how these policies are interpreted and enforced at runtime, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/securing-ai-agents-with-temporal-policies-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Securing AI agents with temporal policies in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/opensource/introducing-dogwood-runtime-verification-for-ai-agents/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Introducing Dogwood: runtime verification for AI agents&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;We would also like to acknowledge the remaining Applied Scientists in our team Chao Shang, Sadat Shahriar, Wanyu Du, and Devang Kulshreshtha for their contributions to this launch.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
